I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two arrays `a` and `b` of the same length `n`.
2. We can perform one transformation on array `a`:
   - Choose `k` distinct indices and add 1 to each of those elements
   - Then permute the resulting array in any order
3. We need to determine if we can transform array `a` into array `b` using exactly one such transformation.

## Key Insights

1. Since we can permute the array after adding 1s, the order of elements doesn't matter in the final comparison.

2. The transformation adds 1 to exactly `k` elements (for some `k` between 0 and `n`), and we can reorder the array freely.

3. This means that after transformation, every element in array `b` must be equal to some element in array `a` plus some non-negative integer (0 or 1), but we need to ensure the total "increment" sums up correctly.

4. Let's think about this differently: 
   - We start with array `a`
   - We add 1 to exactly `k` positions
   - Then we reorder
   - The resulting multiset should be exactly the multiset of `b`

5. A key observation: the multiset of `b` should be obtainable by taking the multiset of `a` and adding 1 to exactly `k` elements (for some `k`).

6. So if we sort both arrays, we can compare them. Since we can reorder, we can sort both arrays and compare them.

7. For the transformation to work:
   - The sorted array `b` should be obtainable from sorted array `a` by adding 1 to exactly `k` elements (where `k` is the number of positions where `b[i] = a[i] + 1`).

8. Actually, a simpler approach: 
   - The difference between corresponding elements in sorted arrays should be 0 or 1, and the sum of differences should be non-negative.
   - But we can only add 1 to elements, not subtract.

Wait, let me reconsider more carefully:

Since we can only add 1 to some elements (not subtract), and then permute, we need:

- The multiset of `b` should be the multiset of `a` with exactly `k` elements increased by 1 (for some `k`).

This means:
- If we sort both arrays, then the elements of `b` should be exactly the elements of `a` with some elements increased by 1.
- In other words, if we sort `a` and sort `b`, then `b` should be a "shifted" version of `a`.

More precisely: 
- Sort both arrays
- For each position `i`, `b[i]` should be `a[i] + d[i]` where `d[i]` is 0 or 1
- The sum of `d[i]` should be between 0 and n
- And `d[i]` should be 0 or 1 for all i

But actually, since we can permute, the condition is that when we sort both arrays, we can find exactly `k` positions where `b[i] = a[i] + 1` and the rest where `b[i] = a[i]`.

However, we also need to ensure that the non-1 differences are 0, and that we're not adding more than we need.

Actually, the simpler approach:
1. Sort both arrays
2. Check if each element in sorted b can be obtained from an element in sorted a by adding 0 or 1
3. Additionally, we need to ensure that the number of elements that are increased by 1 is at most n

But there's a catch: since we can permute, we need to match elements optimally.

The key insight is that after sorting, we can greedily match elements. For each position, if `b[i] > a[i]`, then we need to have `b[i] = a[j] + 1` for some j.

Actually, the cleanest approach:
- Sort both arrays
- Check if for every element in b, there exists an element in a such that b_element = a_element + 0 or 1
- But we can only add to a, so we can't create new values

Wait, let me think of it differently:

The transformation creates a multiset where each element in the original array a is either unchanged or increased by exactly 1.

So we need to check if the multiset of b can be obtained from the multiset of a by increasing exactly k elements by 1 (for some k between 0 and n).

This is equivalent to:
1. Sort both arrays
2. For the multiset transformation: after sorting, the difference between elements should be 0 or 1, and the ones with difference 1 must be exactly the ones that were increased.

But since we're adding 1 to exactly k elements, we can solve this by checking if we can assign a "boost" of 0 or 1 to each element of a such that:
- The sum of boosts is between 0 and n
- The resulting multiset is exactly b

A simpler approach:
- Sort both arrays
- For each position i, check if b[i] - a[i] is 0 or 1
- If not, return NO
- If yes, then the number of positions where b[i] - a[i] = 1 is the number of elements increased, which should be between 0 and n

But this assumes we have the same sorted order. Since we can permute, we need to match optimally.

Actually, since we can permute, we should sort both arrays and then check if we can match them with the constraint that differences are 0 or 1.

Greedy matching: 
- Sort both arrays
- For each element in b (in order), try to match it with an element in a
- The difference should be 0 or 1

But a simpler insight: since we're adding 1 to elements, the sorted b should be greater than or equal to sorted a, and the difference between corresponding elements should be 0 or 1.

Actually, it's sufficient to check:
1. Sort a and b
2. For each i, check that b[i] >= a[i] (since we can only add)
3. For each i, check that b[i] - a[i] is either 0 or 1
4. The number of positions where b[i] - a[i] = 1 is at most n (which it always is since it's at most n)

Wait, the fourth condition is redundant. Since we have n elements, the number of 1s is at most n.

So the algorithm:
- Sort arrays a and b
- For each i from 0 to n-1:
  - If b[i] < a[i]: return "NO"
  - If b[i] > a[i] + 1: return "NO"
- If we pass all checks, return "YES"

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: a = [-1, 1, 0], b = [0, 0, 2]
Sort a: [-1, 0, 1]
Sort b: [0, 0, 2]
i=0: b[0]=0, a[0]=-1, diff = 1, which is 0 or 1 ✓
i=1: b[1]=0, a[1]=0, diff = 0 ✓  
i=2: b[2]=2, a[2]=1, diff = 1 ✓
All good → YES ✓

Example 2: a = [0], b = [2]
Sort a: [0], b: [2]
i=0: b[0]=2, a[0]=0, diff = 2 > 1 → NO ✓

Example 3: a = [1,2,3,4,5], b = [1,2,3,4,5]
Sorted: same, all differences are 0 → YES ✓

It works!

## Solution

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    b = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Sort both arrays
    a.sort()
    b.sort()
    
    # Check if b can be obtained from a by adding 0 or 1 to some elements
    possible = True
    for i in range(n):
        diff = b[i] - a[i]
        if diff < 0:
            possible = False
            break
        if diff > 1:
            possible = False
            break
    
    if possible:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two arrays `a` and `b` of the same length `n`.
2. We can perform exactly one transformation on array `a`:
   - Choose `k` distinct indices (0 ≤ k ≤ n)
   - Add 1 to each of the selected elements
   - Then permute the array elements in any order
3. We need to determine if after this transformation, we can get array `b`.

## Key Insights

1. Since we can permute the array after adding 1s, the order doesn't matter. What matters is the multiset (set with frequencies) of values.

2. The transformation adds 1 to exactly `k` elements of array `a`. So each element in the resulting array is either:
   - The original value from `a` if it's not selected for +1
   - The original value from `a` + 1 if it's selected for +1

3. The key observation: If we sort both arrays, we can compare the frequency distributions.

4. Since we add 1 to exactly `k` elements, the difference between the sorted array `a` and sorted array `b` should be that exactly `k` elements in `b` are exactly 1 more than the corresponding elements in `a`.

5. More precisely: when we sort both arrays, the difference between corresponding elements should be that some elements in `b` are exactly 1 more than elements in `a`.

## Algorithm

1. Sort both arrays `a` and `b`
2. Compare the sorted arrays element by element
3. Count how many positions have `b[i] = a[i] + 1`
4. Count how many positions have `b[i] = a[i]` (these are unchanged)
5. The number of positions where `b[i] = a[i] + 1` should be exactly the number of elements we added 1 to
6. All other elements must match exactly (or be original values)

## Implementation

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    b = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Sort both arrays
    a.sort()
    b.sort()
    
    # Check if each difference is either 0 or 1
    possible = True
    for i in range(n):
        diff = b[i] - a[i]
        if diff < 0 or diff > 1:
            possible = False
            break
    
    if possible:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to determine if we can transform array `a` into array `b` using exactly one transformation operation.

The transformation operation allows us to:
1. Choose any non-negative integer `k` (0 ≤ k ≤ n)
2. Choose `k` distinct indices and add 1 to each of those elements
3. Permute the resulting array in any order

After the transformation, we want the array to be exactly equal to `b`.

## Key Insights

1. Since we can permute the array after the transformation, the order doesn't matter. What matters is the multiset of values.

2. We start with array `a` and add 1 to exactly `k` of its elements (where `k` can be 0 to n).

3. After adding 1 to `k` elements and permuting, we want to get array `b`.

4. The crucial observation: if we can transform `a` into `b`, then `b` must contain exactly the same elements as `a`, but with some elements increased by 1 (exactly `k` elements).

5. More precisely: if we sort both arrays, then the sorted array `b` should be obtainable from sorted array `a` by increasing exactly `k` elements by 1.

6. Since we can only increase values (by adding 1 to selected elements), we can never decrease any value.

7. So for each element in `b`, we should be able to find a corresponding element in `a` such that either:
   - The values are the same, or 
   - The value in `b` is exactly 1 more than the value in `a`

## Algorithm

1. Sort both arrays `a` and `b`.
2. For each element in the sorted `b`, check if we can find a corresponding element in the sorted `a`.
3. Since we're adding 1 to some elements of `a`, we need to check if the multiset of `b` can be formed from the multiset of `a` by increasing exactly some elements by 1.
4. A key insight: we can only increase values, so for each element in `b`, the corresponding element in `a` (in sorted order) should be ≤ the element in `b`.

5. However, we need to be more precise: we need to check if we can form `b` from `a` by increasing some elements by 1.

6. The solution: sort both arrays. Then, for each element in `b`, we need to have a matching element in `a` that is at most that value, and when we add 1 to some elements of `a`, we get exactly the elements of `b`.

7. Actually, we can think of it this way: if we sort both arrays, then the difference between corresponding elements should be 0 or 1, and the total number of 1-differences should be ≤ n.

Wait, that's not quite right either.

Let me reconsider: when we add 1 to k elements of array `a`, we get a new array where k elements are increased by 1, and the rest remain the same. Then we can permute.

So the multiset of values in the transformed array is: the multiset of `a` with k elements increased by 1.

Therefore, for the transformation to be possible:
- The multiset of `b` should be exactly the multiset of `a` with k elements increased by 1, for some k.

This means: if we sort both arrays, then the sorted array `b` should be obtainable from sorted array `a` by increasing k elements by 1.

So the algorithm:
1. Sort both arrays
2. For each element in `b`, we need to pair it with an element in `a` such that either:
   - `b[i] == a[i]`, or 
   - `b[i] == a[i] + 1`

But we need to ensure we can make exactly k such pairs.

Actually, since we can permute, the pairing is flexible. We can match elements in any order.

The correct approach: 
- Sort both arrays
- For each element in `b`, we need to have a matching element in `a` such that the difference is 0 or 1
- But we can only increase elements, not decrease them

So for each element in `b`, the corresponding element in `a` must be ≤ `b[i]`.

However, we also need to ensure that the total "increase" is achievable.

Let me think differently: if we sort both arrays, then we can compare element by element. For each element in `b`, we need to find a corresponding element in `a` such that `a[j] <= b[i]`.

But since the arrays are sorted, we can use a greedy matching.

Actually, the simplest approach:
We can transform `a` into `b` if and only if:
1. We can pair each element of `b` with an element of `a` such that the value in `a` is ≤ the value in `b`
2. And when we do this pairing, the total "increase" (where `b[i] - a[j]`) is achievable with the constraint that we only increase values (and we increase each element at most once).

But since we can only add 1 to elements, the difference between `b[i]` and `a[j]` must be either 0 or 1.

Wait, no - we add 1 to k elements. So if an element in `a` increases by 1, then the difference between the corresponding elements in `a` and `b` is 1. If it stays the same, the difference is 0.

So for each position in the sorted arrays, we need that `b[i] - a[i]` is either 0 or 1, and the sum of all `b[i] - a[i]` should equal exactly k (the number of times we added 1), and k must be ≥ 0 and ≤ n.

But since we can permute, we don't need to match sorted arrays in order. However, if we sort both, we can still use greedy matching.

Actually, since we can permute, we can pair the elements optimally. The condition is that we can achieve the multiset of `b` from the multiset of `a` by increasing some elements by 1.

So: sort both arrays. Then, we can check if the differences in the sorted arrays are non-negative and the sum of differences is at most n.

Wait, let's verify with the examples:

Example 1: a = [-1, 1, 0], b = [0, 0, 2]
Sort a: [-1, 0, 1]
Sort b: [0, 0, 2]

Differences: [0-(-1)=1, 0-0=0, 2-1=1] → differences = [1,0,1], sum = 2
We can increase 2 elements by 1, so k=2, which is ≤ 3. This works.

Example 2: a = [0], b = [2]
Sort a: [0], Sort b: [2]
Difference: [2-0=2], sum = 2 > 1 (n=1), so impossible. This matches.

Example 3: a = [1,2,3,4,5], b = [1,2,3,4,5]
Differences: [0,0,0,0,0], sum = 0 ≤ 5, so possible.

But is the condition "differences sum to at most n" sufficient?

Actually, we also need that no difference is negative, and that we don't need to decrease any values.

So the algorithm:
1. Sort both arrays
2. Calculate differences = b[i] - a[i] for each i
3. Check that all differences ≥ 0
4. Check that sum of differences ≤ n

Is this enough?

Let's think: can we always achieve any non-negative differences as long as their sum is ≤ n?

Actually, we can only add 1 at a time, but we can add to any elements. Since we can permute, we can distribute the increases optimally.

The key insight: for each element in `b`, we need to have an element in `a` to increase. Since we can permute, we can match each element of `b` to an element of `a` such that the value in `a` is ≤ the value in `b`.

The total amount we need to add is sum of (b[i] - a[i]) over all i in sorted order (which is the total "increase").

But since we can only add 1 to elements, we can only add a total of k units where k ≤ n.

Therefore, we need:
- All b[i] ≥ a[i] (after sorting)
- Sum of (b[i] - a[i]) ≤ n

Wait, but is sorting necessary? Could there be a better pairing?

Actually, since we're dealing with multisets and we want to minimize the total increase or something, sorting helps us find a valid pairing.

In fact, for fixed multisets, the sorted order gives us a canonical way to compare.

The total increase needed is at least the sum of (b[i] - a[i]) when both are sorted. If we try to pair differently, we might need even more increases.

Actually, the minimum total increase needed is achieved when we pair sorted arrays. If we pair in a different way, we might get negative differences for some pairs, which is impossible.

So the sorted pairing gives the minimal total increase.

Therefore, the condition is:
- All b[i] ≥ a[i] in sorted order
- Sum of (b[i] - a[i]) ≤ n

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: sorted a = [-1,0,1], sorted b = [0,0,2]
differences: [1,0,1], sum = 2 ≤ 3 → YES

Example 2: sorted a = [0], sorted b = [2]
difference: [2], sum = 2 > 1 → NO

Example 3: differences: [0,0,0,0,0], sum = 0 ≤ 5 → YES

Perfect.

## Implementation

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    b = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Sort both arrays
    a.sort()
    b.sort()
    
    # Calculate differences and check conditions
    total_increase = 0
    valid = True
    
    for i in range(n):
        if b[i] < a[i]:
            valid = False
            break
        total_increase += b[i] - a[i]
    
    if valid and total_increase <= n:
        print("YES")
    else:
        print("NO")
```

Wait, I think I'm missing something. The condition is that we need `b[i] >= a[i]` for all i, and the total increase is at most n.

But let me double-check the logic. We can only increase elements, so each element in `b` must have a corresponding element in `a` that is less than or equal to it.

Since we can permute, we can pair the elements optimally. When we sort both arrays, we're essentially matching the smallest `b` value to the smallest `a` value, etc.

This gives us the minimal total increase needed.

So yes, the algorithm is correct.

t = int(input())
